## Page 2


Abstract. Axial algebras of Monster type (α, β) are a class of commutative
algebras generated by special idempotents called axes. Some motivating exam-
ples of these algebras are the Griess algebra and the Norton-Sakuma algebras,
relating to the Monster simple group.
A long standing open problem is to
classify the 2-generated axial algebras of Monster type (α, β). A huge mile-
stone was accomplished by Yabe leading, with additional cases completed by
Franchi, Mainardis, and McInroy, to the classification in the symmetric case.
In this paper, we complete the classification.
To do so, we split the
proof into multiple cases: dealing with certain parameters, subalgebras, axets,
and axial dimensions.
Furthermore, we provide a basis, multiplication and
information of the algebras in the classification; consolidating existing results
on these algebras into one place.
Keywords: Axial algebras, Griess algebra, Monster Group, Axet.

## Page 5


Introduction
A long standing issue in finite simple groups is finding a unified theory whereby
the sporadic groups could be treated within the same setup as other (possibly all)
classes of finite simple groups, hopefully unveiling new connections between different
classes of finite simple groups.
To this goal, Jon Hall, Felix Rehren, and Sergey Shpectorov introduced in [12]
the class of primitive axial algebras of Monster type (α, β) (in short M(α, β)-axial
algebras, see definition in Section 1.1). This class encompasses several classes of
algebras realising finite simple groups as their automorphism groups.
Including
Jordan algebras, related to the classical groups and G2; Matsuo algebras, related
to the 3-transposition groups; and the Griess algebra, the originating example, and
its subalgebras, related to the Monster sporadic simple group and the sporadic
groups in the Happy Family.
As for other classes of algebras, Lie algebras being a notable example, a key
step towards the understanding and the possible applications of the theory of axial
algebras of Monster type is the classification of the 2-generated objects.
A first, and most important result in this direction was achieved by Simon
Norton, who classified in [26] the 2-generated subalgebras of the Griess algebra.
The Griess algebra can be viewed as the weight 2 component V ♯
(2) of the Moonshine
Module V ♯which is a Vertex Operator Algebra (VOA) that arises as a represen-
tation of the Virasoro algebra (Virasoro VOAs). The class of VOAs was axiom-
atized by Richard Borcherds in [2] in order to give his celebrated solution of the
McKay-Thompson Moonshine conjecture. Using the fact that Virasoro VOAs are
Z2-graded, in 1996 Masahiko Miyamoto introduced Ising vectors for an OZ-type
VOA V and proved that every Ising vector u of V is associated with an involutory
automorphism τu of V , now called a Miyamoto automorphism [25]. In the case of
the Moonshine module (which is a OZ-type VOA), the Ising vectors are precisely
the Conway axes and the Miyamoto automorphisms are the Conway involutions
of the Monster (i.e. those whose centralisers are isomorphic to the double cover of
the Baby Monster). The properties of Ising vectors and Miyamoto automorphisms
turned out to be central in developing the theory of axial algebras. Indeed, based
on Miyamoto’s work, one of his students, Shinya Sakuma, classified the isomor-
phism types of the OZ-type VOA generated by two Ising vectors [29], essentially
obtaining the same classification as Norton’s. For this reason these algebras are now
called Norton-Sakuma algebras. They fall into nine isomorphism classes which are
labelled accordingly to ATLAS notation for the conjugacy class in the Monster of
the product of the Miyamoto involutions associated to the generating Ising vectors.
We display them here as the nodes of McKay’s extended E8 graph:

A detailed description of these and other relevant 2-generated primitive axial alge-
bras of Monster type will be given in Chapter 2.
A careful analysis of Sakuma’s proof, led Alexander A. Ivanov to axiomatize the
class of Majorana algebras, which are particular M( 1
4, 1
32)-axial algebras over the
real numbers [16]. Within these axiomatics Ivanov reproved, in [19], the Norton-
Sakuma classification. Ivanov’s result was later extended in [9] to every M( 1
4, 1
32)-
axial algebra over every field of characteristic 0 and has been effectively used to
study the Monster group and the actions of some of its subgroups on the Griess
Algebra via the theory of Majorana Representations (see [19]).
The investigation of the 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebras over a field F, of
characteristic other than 2, for generic distinct parameters α and β in F \ {0, 1},
was initiated by Rehren in [28] (by an argument of Mathias Stout [30], see also [6,
Lemma 2.1], in characteristic 2 all axial algebras of Monster type are associative,
whence the axial condition brings no relevant information). In [28], Rehren showed
implicitly that we can split into three disjoint cases that behave somewhat differ-
ently (Rehren’s Tricotomy), namely:
(1) α = 2β,
(2) α = 4β,
(3) all other cases (the regular case),
and showed that, in the regular case, these algebras have dimension at most 8.
A major breakthrough was due to Takahiro Yabe, who in [34] gave an almost
complete classification of the 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebras (V, {a0, a1}), un-
der the assumption that there exists an involutory automorphism (called a flip)
of the algebra V that swaps the two generating axes a0 and a1. In this case the
M(α, β)-axial algebra (V, {a0, a1}) is called symmetric. The remaining subcases of
the symmetric case left open by Yabe were eventually completed in [3, 5].
Classification Theorem of the Symmetric Algebras ([34, 3, 5]). Let
F be a field of characteristic other than 2, α, β ∈F \ {0, 1} with α ̸= β, and
(V, {a0, a1}) be a (primitive) symmetric 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebra. Then
(V, {a0, a1}) is isomorphic to a quotient of one of the following:
(a) an axial algebra of Jordan type α, or β;
(b) an axial algebra in one of the following families:
(i) 3A(α, β), 4A( 1

In this paper, we complete the classification of the 2-generated M(α, β)-axial
algebras by considering the non-symmetric algebras and prove the following result
which gives a complete answer to [15, §10, Question (c)] and [24, Problem 5.15].
Main Theorem. Let F be a field of characteristic other than 2, α, β ∈F\{0, 1}
with α ̸= β, and (V, {a0, a1}) be a (primitive) 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebra
over F. Then (V, {a0, a1}) is either symmetric or isomorphic to one of the following:
(a) Q2(2β, β);
(b) Q2(−1, −1

For more information and motivation on axial algebras and Majorana algebras,
we refer to the survey papers [24, 17, 18, 4]. In particular, [24] is a good place to
find out who first constructed the algebras mentioned in this paper.
We now state some consequences of the Main Theorem.
Corollary 1. All (primitive) 2-generated axial algebras of Monster type admit
a Frobenius form.
This supports the conjecture that every axial algebra of Monster type admits
a Frobenius form (see [24, Conjecture 6.1]). This has been proven for every axial
algebra of Jordan type in [14, Theorem 4.1], where in addition, it is shown that the
Frobenius form may be chosen in such a way that all axes have length 1. The same
holds for 2-generated symmetric axial algebras of Monster type. On the contrary,
in the non-symmetric algebras axes may have different lengths, even 0 in a couple
of cases (see Table 2.17).
The algebras in the Main Theorem can fail to be symmetric in three different
ways. Firstly, the axet may be skew: both 3C′(α, 1 −α) and Q′
2( 1
3, 2
3) have skew
axet X′(3). The remaining algebras have a regular axet. Secondly, the generating
axes can have different lengths (the projections in these cases are also not equal).
This occurs in Q2(2β, β) and Q2(−1, −1
2)×. Finally, even if the axes have the same
length, we may fail to have an algebra automorphism, as in 4B(−1, 1
2; ν)×.

Overview
In Chapter 1, we give the basic definitions and properties of 2-generated axial
algebras, in particular that of the universal algebra V, and prove several general
lemmas.
In Chapter 2, we describe all the 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebras and the
properties of these algebras which are relevant for this paper, including the sym-
metric and non-symmetric algebras.
The proof of the Main Theorem is given in Chapters 3-7 and follows the scheme
given in Table 0.1, which we shall now describe.

If the RF-algebra V is generated (as an algebra) by a set A of primitive F-axes,
then we say that the pair V := (V, A) is an F-axial algebra over RF. We call the
RF-algebra V the support of V and will be also denoted by supp(V). If F = J (η)
(respectively F = M(α, β)), then we say that V is of Jordan type η (respectively
Monster type (α, β)). We remark that, as a difference to the definition of F-algebra
given in this paper, in the existing literature the condition of primitivity is not
always required.
A Frobenius form on an F-axial algebra V over RF is a non-zero bilinear form
κ: V × V →RF which is invariant with respect to the algebra product, that is, for
every u, v, w ∈V ,
κ(uv, w) = κ(u, vw).
The invariance of the Frobenius form κ implies that for every axis a of V , the
eigenspaces of ada are mutually orthogonal.
In particular it follows that the
Miyamoto involution τa is an isometry.
Moreover, since V is commutative and
generated by idempotents, invariance also implies that κ is symmetric.
Let V := (V, A) be an M(α, β)-axial algebra over RF. The Miyamoto group
of V is the group generated by the Miyamoto involutions associated to the axes in
A and will be denoted by Miy(V). Note that, if η ∈{α, β}, then, by Table 1.1,
every axial algebra V of Jordan type η is also an M(α, β)-axial algebra. Precisely,
if η = α, then V a
β = {0} for every a ∈A and Miy(V) is trivial. If η = β, then
V a
α = {0} for every a ∈A and Miy(V) is trivial if and only if also V a
β = {0} for
every a ∈A, whence, by [9, Lemma 3.3], V is associative.
Given two F-axial algebras V := (V, A) and W := (W, B) over RF, we say that
an RF-algebra homomorphism
φ: V →W
is an axial homomorphism if
Aφ ⊆B.
If φ is also bijective, we say that V and W are isomorphic. Note that the M(α, β)-
axial algebras 3C(α) and 3C′(α, 1 −α) have isomorphic supports but are not iso-
morphic as axial algebras (see Note 2 to Table 2.17).

Lemma 1.11. If a0 = a2 or a1 = a−1, then either V is an algebra of Jordan type
(and in particular it is symmetric) or V has a skew axet (and Theorem D holds).
Proof. Suppose a0 = a2. Then, by Corollary 1.10 (b) (and using the same
notation), n0 = 1 and n1 ∈{1, 2}. If n1 = 2, then V has a skew axet and Theorem D
holds. If n1 = 1, then a1 = a−1, and so τ0 and τ1 are the identity on V . This implies
that the β-eigenspaces of ada0 and ada1 are trivial. Hence V is a 2-generated algebra
of Jordan type and, by [13], it is symmetric.
□
1.2. The universal M(α, β)-axial algebra V
As in the previous one, in this section F is a field of characteristic other than 2.
By [9, Theorem 5.8 and Corollary 5.10] there exist a ring ˜F containing F as a subring
and a 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebra (the universal M(α, β)-axial algebra) V :=
(V, {a0, a1}) over the ring ˜F, such that, for every 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebra
V := (V, {a0, a1}) over the field F, there is a unique surjective evaluation map
(depending on V)

of F-algebra by restricting the ring of scalars,

1.4. Evaluation in V
Let V := (V, {a0, a1}) be a 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebra over F. Since, by
Equation (1.8) on page 16, V is a homomorphic image of V and F is a homomorphic
image of ˜F, Equations (1.10)-(1.22) still hold in the algebra V , once ai is replaced
by ai, s¯r,j by s¯r,j, and every coefficient in ˜F by its image under φ.
It is important to note that the map
F −→F
Zφ 7−→(Zf)φ
where Z ∈˜F, is not necessarily an automorphism of F.2
In order to simplify notation, when it is clear from the context, we shall omit
the superscript φ and use the same symbol Z to denote both an element Z in ˜F

## Page 23


CHAPTER 2
Known algebras
In this chapter we describe the algebras appearing in the Main Theorem. More-
over, we obtain some properties of the symmetric algebras that are needed in the
proof of the Main Theorem.
More information on these algebras can be found
in [5, 6, 13, 21, 22, 23]. As throughout this paper, F is a field of characteristic
other than 2 and α and β are distinct elements in F \ {0, 1}.
2.1. The 2-generated axial algebras of Jordan type
The 2-generated algebras of Jordan type are, up to isomorphism, the algebras
1A, 2B, 3C(η), its quotient 3C(−1)× for η = −1, J(δ) and its quotient J(0)× for
δ = 0 (see [13]). There are listed in Table 2.4 in Section 2.4, where a basis, the
structure constants and the relevant values of the Frobenius form are given (recall
that all algebras considered in this paper are commutative and the bilinear form ( , )
is always symmetric). Note that, except for the cases 1A and 2B, each isomorphism
class of 2-generated J (η)-axial algebras splits into two distinct isomorphism classes
of M(α, β)-axial algebras, according whether η = α or η = β. All these algebras
are symmetric and afford an invariant Frobenius form.
In the next lemmas we describe some features of the algebras of Table 2.4,
when considered as M(α, β)-axial algebras with β = η.
Note that in this case
the β-eigenspaces of the adjoint maps ada0 and ada1 are non-trivial, whence the
Miyamoto groups of the algebras are non-trivial.
Lemma 2.1. Let V = 2B. The following assertions hold:
(a) λ1 = 0;
(b) a2 = a0;
(c) V = V ∗;
(d) V ∗∗= {0}.
Proof. As a1 is a 0-eigenvector of ada0, (a) is true. Notice ada0 and ada1 only
have eigenvalues of 1 and 0; thus τ0 and τ1 are trivial and (b) follows. Part (d)
follows immediately from (b).
For (c), notice that (a0 −a1)2 = a0 + a1 and so a0, a1 ∈V ∗.
□

2; µ) or IY3(2, 1
2; 1)×;

i=0 αiai
with α0 ̸= 0 ̸= αd, Pd
i=0 αi = 0, and αi = εαd−i for every i ∈{0, . . . , d}, ε = ±1.

2; µ), with δ = −2µ −1. If ε = 1, then up

(see [5, Table 28]). Finally, by Lemma 2.15, the proper quotients of IH3 with axial

If I is an ideal of an algebra A, then clearly every idempotent e ∈A induces
an idempotent ¯e (possibly trivial) of A/I. The following examines when we can lift
idempotents of A/I to idempotents of A.
Lemma 4.2. Let A be an algebra, I ⊴A such that I ≤Ann(A) and e ∈A. If ¯e
is an idempotent of A/I, then ¯e lifts to a unique idempotent in A.
Proof. Since ¯e is an idempotent of A/I, there exists r ∈I such that e2 = e+r.
Then,
(e + r)2 = e2 = e + r
and so the lift e + r of ¯e is an idempotent of A. Now, assume that e + s is another
idempotent in A, where s ∈I. Then
e + s = (e + s)2 = e2 = e + r
and so s = r.
□

Proof of Theorem E. Let V be an axial algebra satisfying the conditions
of Theorem E. By Theorems A, D, and Lemma 1.11, we may assume that V satisfy
Hypothesis 4.1. By Lemma 1.7, Ve is a symmetric M(α, β)-axial algebra. By the
Classification Theorem of the Symmetric Algebras, there is a complete list of what
Ve is. By Lemma 4.3, if Ve is an axial algebra of Jordan type, then V is symmetric.
By Lemma 4.4 and 4.5, if Ve is isomorphic to either IY3(α, 1
2; µ), IY5(α, 1
2), or any
of their quotients, then V is symmetric. By Corollary 4.8, if Ve is isomorphic to a
quotient of ˆH, then V is symmetric. Finally, Lemmas 4.9– 4.14 show that Ve cannot
be any of the remaining algebras. Since these cases exhaust all possibilities of Ve,
the result follows.
□

CHAPTER 6
Algebras with regular axet
In this chapter we prove Theorems F, G, and H. By Theorems A, C, D, E we can
restrict ourselves to studying the 2-generated M(α, β)-axial algebras V satisfying
the following conditions:
Hypothesis 6.1.
(1) α ̸= 2β,
(2) V is not isomorphic to a quotient of a symmetric algebra,
(3) V ̸∈{Ve, Vo},
(4) V has axet X(n + n) for n ∈N ∪{∞} and n ≥2.
Remark 6.2. Hypotheses 6.1(3) and (4) are equivalent to saying that Ve and
Vo are distinct and both have axet X(n).
6.1. Algebras with axet X(2 + 2)
In this section we assume that V satisfies Hypothesis 6.1 with n = 2.

## Page 103


CHAPTER 7
Proofs of the Main Theorem and its corollaries
Proof of the Main Theorem. For the sake of contradiction, suppose V is
an algebra that is not listed in the Theorem. By Theorems A and D, α ̸= 2β and V
does not have a skew axet. Therefore V has a regular axet of size 2n with n ≥1. By
Lemma 1.11 and Theorems E, F, G, and H, we get the desired contradiction.
□
Proof of Corollaries 1 and 2. This follows by direct inspection using the
tables in Section 2.4.
□
Proof of Corollary 3. Recall that a 6-transposition group is a group gen-
erated by a normal set T of involutions such that the product of every two elements
of T has order less or equal to 6 (see [33, §5.8.3]). Taking T = X, with X as in
Equation (0.1), the result follows by Corollary 2 with β ̸= 1
2, since every subalgebra
generated by two axes in X will have axet of size less than or equal to 6.
□
